Your wallet's rat peeks over the top, and Post on X shares the receipt. Nothing to connect, no sign-in. Live now.\n\n- Closed trades list straight away. A coin you sell out of now shows in Portfolio › Closed at once. Before, it disappeared from both Open and Closed for 30 minutes. Live now.\n\n- Open bags stop going blank. If the price source refused one coin, every bag in that wallet lost its price and balance. Now only that coin goes unpriced. Coins you sold or sent away outside Exfil leave Open instead of sitting there with \"—\", and totals and leaderboards still count their trades. Before this, 51 of 300 open rows across all traders showed \"—\". This fixes 43 of them; the other 8 are dead pools and say \"Price unavailable\". Live now.\n\n- Find the buy behind a routed trade. That was 8 of the top 30 holders on TORCH (Robinhood Chain), 10 of 30 on a Base coin and 14 of 30 on a BNB Chain coin. Exfil now counts the coin arriving in the wallet in that same transaction as the holder's buy, at that fill's price, so their entry, place on the hill and hold history show up. This works on Robinhood Chain, Base and BNB Chain. A plain wallet-to-wallet transfer still isn't counted as a buy, and Robinhood Chain's burn address now reads \"Burn address\". Weekly leagues now value a Robinhood, Base or BNB Chain coin with no market at $0, the same as a Solana coin, so a drained pool no longer counts as a win. Live now.\n\n- Your rat's name follows you. Takes show under holder rows, at the top of the poster's rat sheet, and in a Takes tile on the strip that flies the camera to each poster. Each person gets one live take per coin. A trader we spoke with couldn't tell what the rats meant.\n\n- Know the sides before you buy. Before you Drop in, the race line describes the top 100 holders in plain words: \"▲ 31 in profit · ▼ 64 underwater\". \"Ahead\" and \"behind\" only mean something once you hold, so after you buy, the line switches to ahead and behind your entry, as before.\n\n- The hill takes the coin's mood. When a coin is up 15%+ in the hour, the field turns gold and the rats rise on their toes. On a fall, the field goes cold and the rats hunch. A coin 70%+ below its peak with a flat hour sits at dusk. A sharp 5-minute drop now shows in the header (\"−14.8% · Past 5 min\"), so the field never goes cold under a green hour. https://t.co/INF2NF9woY","url":"https://x.com/america/status/2113299342186426434"},{"author_id":"1874492187070615552","conversation_id":"2112612285499732170","created_at":"2026-10-20T18:29:53.000Z","edit_history_tweet_ids":["2112612285499732170"],"entities":{"urls":[{"display_url":"x.com/TheRabbitHole/…","end":302,"expanded_url":"https://twitter.com/TheRabbitHole/status/2112605691454374231","start":279,"url":"https://t.co/RJjZGnakty"}]},"id":"2112612285499732170","lang":"en","note_tweet":{"text":"Capitalism has a win condition. Socialism does not.\n\nHere's why, explained so simply a 10-year-old could get it. It starts with a book written in 1944 by an Austrian economist named Friedrich Hayek: The Road to Serfdom.\n\nImagine a classroom of 30 kids. One day the teacher says: \"From now on, I decide what everyone eats for lunch, which games you play at recess, and who sits next to whom. It's fairer that way.\"\n\nFirst problem: the teacher doesn't know what you like. You hate carrots. Your friend is allergic to peanuts. Lucas wants to play football, Emma wants to draw. 30 kids, 30 different wishes. Nobody can know all of that, not even the smartest teacher on Earth. Hayek called this the knowledge problem. The information needed to run an economy is scattered across millions of heads, and it can't be gathered in one office.\n\nSecond problem: the kids start fighting. \"Why does he get the window seat?\" \"Why football again?\" Before, everyone just chose for themselves. Now every choice is a fight, because every choice is made for everyone.\n\nThird problem: the class can't agree on a plan, so someone says \"we need one person in charge who just decides.\" This is Hayek's key insight. Planning doesn't fail because planners are evil. A plan needs everyone to agree on the same goals, and free people never agree on everything. So the plan needs force to work.\n\nFourth problem, and it's the scariest one: who wants that job? The kind kid doesn't want it, because the kind kid doesn't want to force 29 people to eat carrots. The job attracts the one who likes giving orders and doesn't mind punishing those who disobey. Hayek wrote a whole chapter on this, called \"Why the Worst Get on Top.\" It doesn't happen by accident. It happens by selection.\n\nHere's the trap. When the person who controls your lunch also controls your job, your home, your shop and your newspaper, they control your whole life. You can't say no, because there's nowhere else to go. Controlling the economy doesn't mean controlling one part of life. It means controlling the means to every goal you have.\n\nThat's the road to serfdom. Nobody votes for a dictator. People vote for \"someone should fix this,\" then for \"give them more power to fix it,\" and one day they wake up with no one left to say no to.\n\nThe USSR, Maoist China, Cambodia, North Korea, Cuba, Venezuela. These are different countries with different cultures and languages, and the story has the same ending every time. When a script produces the same ending every time, it isn't bad luck. It's the script.\n\nNow capitalism. Back to the classroom. Instead of one teacher deciding everything, every kid can trade. You give me your carrots, I give you my apple, and we both walk away happier. Nobody needs to know what everyone wants, because prices do that job. A price is a message telling millions of strangers what's scarce and what's wanted, with no one in charge.\n\nThat's the win condition. In capitalism, you get rich by making something other people freely choose to buy, so your success depends on serving others. In a planned system, you get ahead by pleasing whoever holds power. One rewards usefulness. The other rewards obedience.\n\nAnd this isn't theory. Two centuries ago, almost every human being was extremely poor. In 1990, about 38% of humanity still lived in extreme poverty. Today it's under 10%. More than a billion people escaped it in a single generation. It happened when China, India, Vietnam and South Korea opened up to markets, trade and private enterprise. The largest reduction in poverty in human history came from letting people trade.\n\nSo why do so many people hate capitalism?\n\nBecause what they hate usually isn't capitalism.\n\nThey hate the bank that got bailed out with their taxes while they lost their house. They hate the giant company that writes the regulations so no startup can compete with it. They hate rent they can't afford because building new homes has been made nearly impossible. They hate the lobbyist who gets a subsidy while the small business owner drowns in paperwork.\n\nLook closely at each one, and you'll find a state handing out privileges. A bailout is not the market. The market would have let the bank fail. A regulation written by incumbents is not competition. It's protection from competition. None of this is capitalism. It's cronyism, which is what happens when businesses stop competing for customers and start competing for politicians.\n\nPeople see the symptom and blame the wrong cause. They say \"capitalism failed\" and ask for more state. That gives more power to the same people who created the problem, and it puts us right back on Hayek's road.\n\nThe answer to bad capitalism isn't less freedom. It's less privilege. That means fewer bailouts, fewer protected incumbents, fewer permits, and more people allowed to build, compete and trade.\n\nCapitalism has a win condition: make things people want. Socialism has an end condition: someone has to be in charge. And history keeps showing us who ends up in that chair."},"possibly_sensitive":false,"public_metrics":{"bookmark_count":2034,"impression_count":253334,"like_count":3963,"quote_count":110,"reply_count":105,"repost_count":1162},"referenced_tweets":[{"id":"2112605691454374231","type":"quoted"}],"text":"Capitalism has a win condition. But recently, one has been the most common of them all. Oil prices are above $100/barrel, borrowing costs are at 2002 levels, and inflation is nearly double the Fed's 2% target. So, HOW is the S&P 500 trading ~2% away from a record high?\nWhat if we told you that the S&P 500 can trade near record highs, while simultaneously being in a bear market?\nThat is exactly what we believe is happening. And we believe it represents one of the most significant opportunities in today's market.\nWe are now witnessing a historic market paradox: The \"Bull-Bear\" Market.\nBelow, we explain exactly what we mean.\nThe Iran War - Day #229\n \nToday marks the 229th day of the Iran War. Since the start of the Iran War, the S&P 500 is up +900 points. And, since the March 30th bottom, the S&P 500 is up nearly +1,500 points, adding +$14 trillion of market cap.\nThe world is also a vastly different place than it was 229 days ago. Inflation has nearly doubled, the Fed is hiking interest rates, and we are in the midst of the biggest energy crisis in world history. And, we are also in the midst of the biggest technological revolution in world history. \nYet, if you ask most investors, the current sentiment is that the current fundamental backdrop has deteriorated compared to where it was 8 months ago. \nThe Macroeconomy - A Historic Shift In Expectations \nAt the start of 2026, the Fed was expected to CUT rates at least 3 times, for a total of 75 basis points this year. \n \nThis was a major component of the bullish thesis for equities, with investors expecting cheaper capital, more investment, and record CapEx by technology companies.\n \nToday, the Fed has already hiked interest rates once this year and markets see another 3 interest rate hikes by June 2027, for a total of +100 basis points including September's rate hike. That's a +175 bps swing in rate expectations. Yet, the S&P 500 is still trading at record highs.  \n \nDriving this shift has been the historic surge in oil prices, with diesel now up over +80% from its low seen in January 2026 and Brent crude oil trading at $106/barrel. \nBorrowing costs have surged as inflation doubled, the Fed is hiking rates, and the US is running a near-$2 trillion deficit. But still, the S&P 500 is at record highs. \nSo, what is happening? \nMost Of The S&P 500 Is Struggling: \nThe reality is that the vast majority of the S&P 500's constituents are not trading anywhere near record high territory. Take a look at our analysis below.  \n \nAs indicated in the chart above, 84% of S&P 500 stocks are now trading at least -10% below their all-time highs. \nReal Estate leads the drawdown, with 97% of stocks down at least -10%, followed by Materials at 96%, Utilities at 94%, and Consumer Discretionary at 92%. \nFurthermore, 59% of S&P 500 stocks are down at least -20% from their highs, 41% are down more than -30%, and 17% are down more than -50%.\nNow, take a look at return contribution since the March 30th bottom: \n \nJust 10 stocks have driven 70% of the S&P 500’s gain since March 30th.  These stocks have added +16 percentage points to the index’s +23% gain over this period. Nvidia, $NVDA, has contributed the most, accounting for 13% of the S&P 500's gain, or +3 percentage points.\nThis is followed by Micron, $MU, Apple, $AAPL, and Microsoft, $MSFT, each accounting for 9% of the S&P 500’s advance, adding +2 percentage points. Return contributions by AMD, $AMD, and Alphabet, $GOOGL, have been 8% and 7%, respectively.\nMeanwhile, the remaining 490 stocks have accounted for just 30% of the S&P 500's gain since March 30th, adding +7 percentage points. \nThe reality is that the MAJORITY of S&P 500 components are, in fact, in a bear market. In our view, for the S&P 500, this is in fact bullish, or at worst, does not matter for now. \nWe explain why, next. \nThe S&P 500 Should Be Above 9,000: \nWithout the Iran War, the S&P 500 could be at 9,000+. \nThis is arguably the most resilient market in history. If the S&P 500 is 2% away from a record high despite inflation nearing 4%, gas prices on the rise, and the Fed HIKING rates, just imagine where the S&P 500 would be if the Iran War ended, oil prices came back down, and rate cuts returned. Or, if it never even happened.\nEarnings growth just over the last 7 months of the Iran War has been historic, with the S&P 500 posting +52% blended earnings growth in Q2 2026.  \n \nThis marks the highest growth rate since Q2 2021, a period where the global economy was emerging from a historic lockdown and the US government handed out $4 trillion in stimulus. \nIn other words, in the midst of a global energy crisis and a rate hike campaign, we are now seeing growth on par with $4+ trillion in stimulus and a global economy that restarted from an effective halt. \nThe reality is that the AI narrative is even stronger than most people realize: the earnings growth is there to support it, even as inflation has been above 2% for 67 consecutive months.  \n \nTake a look at Nvidia's P/E ratio over the last 12 months. Even as the stock is up +21% over this period, the company's P/E ratio is down -46%. The stock now has a Forward P/E ratio of ~24x, nearly in line with the S&P 500's 22x, even as $NVDA has risen +900% in 5 years. \nThis is the story for MANY large-cap technology stocks, the very names that are carrying this market. \nAs shown below, the S&P 500 and the S&P 500 Information Technology sector are now seeing nearly identical Forward P/E ratios. Historically, these stocks have almost always traded at a premium to the broader S&P 500.  \n \nIn our view, the very names that have been driving the equity market higher, most large-cap technology stocks, actually have deep discounts priced-in to their stocks, even as they trade at or near record highs. We believe these stocks would be trading MUCH higher if it were not for the macroeconomic factors that we have seen over the last seven months. \nCombine this with the fact that over 80% of S&P 500 stocks are down -10% or more from their record highs, and our thesis is even stronger. If market breadth were to widen even slightly, buying pressure increases rapidly. \nAnd, even without this shift, the large-cap technology stocks that are driving the majority of price action appear relatively \"cheap\" compared to the S&P 500. \nThis is why we said for the S&P 500, market concentration is bullish, or at worst, does not matter *for now.* \nMarch 30th Was A Gift To Investors: \nThe thesis we outline today has been a central part of our investment strategy in our premium research since March 30th. Below is the front page of The Kobeissi Letter as published on March 30th, 2026.  \n \nAs the S&P 500 fell below 6,400, we initiated a strong buy signal on the index and the equity market more broadly. Below is our note on the S&P 500 from March 30th.  \n \nWe noted that the bond market was a central part of our thesis along with the fact that the technology stocks that were driving the broader equity market lower were being sold with seemingly irrational price action. \nThe result was a \"V-Shaped\" recovery in the S&P 500, after which we continuously raised our S&P 500 target to 7,800, and we also called for bond market intervention by the Trump Administration. \nEvery Sunday, we update the above analysis in our weekly research and we also provide daily updates on our private X feed. If you are not already a client, consider subscribing to receive analysis like this in real-time here.\nToday, the Trump Administration is intervening in the bond market, so far unsuccessfully, but further attempts at intervention are inevitable in our view. \nUltimately, however, our thesis today is rooted in our view expressed on March 30th, which has only gained more momentum as we look ahead to 2027. \nInvestors Want More Assets: \nOn top of the discount that is already priced-in to equity markets as discussed above, investors are keen to own assets amid the persistent inflation we are seeing in the US. \nUS CPI inflation has now been above the Fed's 2% target for 67 consecutive months. The last time inflation was below 2% in the US was in January 2021, amid a global economic lockdown. \n \nOver this period, the value of the US Dollar has declined by -22.5%. In other words, it now costs $129 to purchase the same goods and services that cost $100 in 2021. Hence, investors want more stocks and hard assets over the long-run, not less. \nTherefore, if there were to be any indication of the Iran War ending and the global energy crisis subsiding, this would lead to a domino effect in the bullish direction. \nEnergy prices would drop, inflation expectations would follow, the Fed could pivot more dovish, the \"discount\" in technology stocks would be priced-out, and the market's rally would broaden. \nAll while the AI Revolution continues to accelerate and many large-cap technology stocks become cheaper on a forward earnings basis, even as their stock prices rise. \nAnd in the background, the demand for asset ownership is historically robust.\nIn Conclusion: The \"Bull-Bear Market.\" \nIn summary, we believe the S&P 500 is in a bear market, within a bull market.  \n \nEven as the S&P 500 hits year #5 of its bull market, we believe a steep discount is currently priced into equities. This discount has functioned as a \"stealth\" bear market, suppressing equity valuations in the world's largest and fastest-growing companies. \nThis backdrop has the ability to catalyze a violent rally in the S&P 500 if and when it is ultimately priced-out. \nWe expect a highly eventful 2027. \nFollow us @KobeissiLetter for real time analysis as this develops. \nMore about our strategy: Those who are able to remain objective and follow a process during current times of disruption are realizing some of the best trading conditions ever. \nAn objective and systematic approach is what has led to our outperformance of market benchmarks. \nAs shown below, our investment strategy has returned nearly five times the S&P 500 since 2020.  \n \nIf you are interested in receiving our premium daily analysis, you may do so by joining our service here.","preview_text":"We receive thousands of questions per week. people say the Chosen One prophecy came out of nowhere in the prequels. They say it feels tacked-on and has nothing to do with the story of the original films. \n\nActually, some of Lucas’s earliest notes for 𝘛𝘩𝘦 𝘌𝘮𝘱𝘪𝘳𝘦 𝘚𝘵𝘳𝘪𝘬𝘦𝘴 𝘉𝘢𝘤𝘬 show him conceptualizing Luke as “the chosen one.” This is an obvious development of the famous “son of the suns” prophecy from the “Journal of the Whills,” which appeared in the second and third drafts of the first film. But who cares, right? These are just early ideas. This was all dropped, it's not in 𝘛𝘩𝘦 𝘌𝘮𝘱𝘪𝘳𝘦 𝘚𝘵𝘳𝘪𝘬𝘦𝘴 𝘉𝘢𝘤𝘬.\n\nWell, no. It is in the 𝘛𝘩𝘦 𝘌𝘮𝘱𝘪𝘳𝘦 𝘚𝘵𝘳𝘪𝘬𝘦𝘴 𝘉𝘢𝘤𝘬:\n\n“He could destroy us... The son of Skywalker must not become a Jedi.”\n\nThis is a prophecy. In the original trilogy, the Emperor famously has the gift of prophecy. He foresees everything. That is why the specific, almost Biblical title “son of Skywalker” is given such prominent placement in this scene, pronounced by the Emperor with such an ethereal air. Luke is not the Chosen One or the Son of the Suns. He is the Son of Skywalker, and a prophecy is literally being made about him here: 𝘐𝘧 he becomes a Jedi, he can destroy Vader and the Emperor.\n\nYoda echoes the Emperor’s prophecy later in the film, when he’s begging Luke not to leave:  \n\n“Stopped they must be. On this all depends. Only a fully trained Jedi Knight with the Force as his ally will conquer Vader and his Emperor.”\n\nVader repeats it in the reactor shaft on Cloud City, but he twists it, telling Luke that in order to fulfill the prophecy, he must become the son of Vader:\n\n“Luke. You can destroy the Emperor. He has foreseen this. It is your destiny. Join me, and together we can rule the galaxy as father and son.”\n\nIn the next film, Yoda makes another prophecy on his deathbed:\n\n“You must confront Vader. Then, only then, a Jedi will you be. And confront him you will.”\n\nSo, according to Yoda, only a fully trained Jedi Knight can conquer Vader and his Emperor; yet Luke can only become a Jedi through confrontation with Vader, his father. And Luke 𝘸𝘪𝘭𝘭 confront his father. \n\nSo what does all this mean? How can Luke defeat Vader and the Emperor if he has to be a Jedi to do it, when the only way for him to become a Jedi in the first place is through confrontation with Vader? \n\nIt can only mean one possible thing, and it explains how Obi-Wan and Yoda ever expected Luke to be able to defeat the Emperor at that point. It was because they knew that the son of Skywalker, in becoming a Jedi, could somehow redeem his father through confrontation, and his 𝘧𝘢𝘵𝘩𝘦𝘳 would then destroy the Emperor and himself. The Emperor misread his own prophecy, but the Jedi had their own prophecy all along.  \n\nAnd everything in the Jedi prophecy comes to pass: Luke said he would not confront his father; but he did. Luke said he could not kill his father; but he could. Yet he didn’t, and in that moment he became a Jedi, setting off the exact sequence of events which were required to finally redeem his father.\n\nThe obvious corollary to all this is that Darth Vader, or Anakin Skywalker, was the actual prophetic subject. That's the twist. So if Luke is the Son of Skywalker, who is “Skywalker”? Who is Luke the son of? Lucas simply gave Vader the title he had previously considered giving to Luke, before it became clear to him that Vader should be the one to destroy the Emperor: the Chosen One. And he decided to extend this element beyond the Emperor’s power of prophecy, and reveal that there was a larger prophecy operating in the background of the older characters’ motivations. \n\nThis is an embellishment, which is not a crime for a prequel in any case, but it actually slots into the original narrative quite well because of all this. I spend an enormous amount of time trying to understand the chaos we're living through. Not the headlines. The machine behind them. 🚨\n\nIf you want to understand what Elon Musk is really fighting, and who is really fighting him, this is the clearest map I can give you.\n\nExpensive houses. Expensive schools. Expensive healthcare. Taxes that rise every year while everything gets worse. Borders nobody voted to open. Speech rules nobody voted for.\n\nMost people look at this and blame capitalism. The market failed, they say, so the state has to step in and fix it.\n\nThey have it exactly backwards. What they want to fix it with is what broke it. This isn't chaos, and it isn't capitalism. It has a direction, a method, and a hundred-year history. Let me reverse-engineer it.\n\nIn 1926, Mussolini's regime threw Antonio Gramsci, co-founder of the Italian Communist Party, into prison. From his cell he asked the question that would shape the next century: why did the revolution win in Russia but fail in the West, where Marx said it was inevitable?\n\nHis answer: in Russia, the state was everything, so seizing it was enough. In the West, the state was only the outer wall. Behind it stood the real fortress: the church, the family, the school, the press, the culture. Western workers never revolted because the culture held them.\n\nSo the conclusion was simple. Don't storm the palace. Capture the culture. Win the institutions and you never have to win the argument, because you decide which arguments are allowed.\n\nIn 1967, the German student leader Rudi Dutschke gave it a slogan: the long march through the institutions. Paris supplied the intellectual software, and I've already apologized for my country's part in that.\n\nThen came the most patient operation in modern history. The students of the 60s became the professors of the 80s. Their students became the journalists, judges, civil servants and HR directors of the 2000s. Their students now run the foundations, the NGOs and the agencies. No single step looked like a revolution. That's exactly why it worked.\n\nThe method was communist. The ambition became global. And it grew like a cancer. A cancer never attacks the body head-on. It hijacks the body's own cells and feeds on the body's own resources. This system never had to defeat the West. It just had to make the West pay for it.\n\nIn America, the machine found a political home: the Democratic Party. Not the millions of decent people who vote for it. The apparatus. The donor networks, the activist groups, the consultants, the captured agencies that keep moving in the same direction whoever wins.\n\nIn Europe, it found something even better. The EU's founders, Schuman, Adenauer, De Gasperi, were Catholic anti-communists who wanted to make war impossible. But they built exactly what the long march dreams of: power that never has to face a voter. A Commission nobody directly elects holds the right to propose laws for 450 million people. Chat Control. The digital euro. Speech rules written in Brussels. Nobody stormed anything. They just drafted it.\n\nAdd the World Economic Forum, the UN agencies and the global foundation circuit, and you have the full map. You don't need a secret meeting when everyone went to the same schools, reads the same papers, funds the same NGOs and flies to the same conference in Davos every January. It's not a conspiracy. It's a class with shared incentives. That's harder to fight, because there's no headquarters to raid.\n\nAnd the billionaires? Most people who became the richest in the world got absorbed. They built something real, then traded it for an aura. Bill Gates is the perfect example: he built the software that ran the world, then became the bankroller of global health bureaucracies and the funder of newsrooms covering his own causes. Laurene Powell Jobs bought The Atlantic. Benioff bought Time. You're not buying a business. You're buying the right to be called good by the people who decide who is good. That's the corruption. Not suitcases of cash. Money for aura, grants for loyalty, NGOs for policy. All legal. All invisible.\n\nHere's the proof it's one system and not a hundred separate problems. Look at what happened to prices over the last 25 years. Everything exposed to markets and technology got cheaper: TVs, software, phones, clothing. Everything controlled by institutions exploded: college, healthcare, childcare. And housing, where you need a committee's permission to build anything. Same country, same economy, same decades. The difference isn't the product. It's who controls it.\n\nTaxes go up and services get worse, because the money doesn't reach the services. It feeds the layers. Immigration happens at a scale no electorate clearly chose, because for a system that lives on managing problems, more problems means more budget. And a two-income family today can't buy the house their parents bought on one salary. That's real poverty, manufactured by a machine that is rewarded for looking good, never for being good.\n\nMAGA isn't the cause of the chaos. It's the immune response. Brexit, the farmers' revolts, the votes shaking every European capital: same thing. The body finally noticed the tumor.\n\nSo what we're living through is not a normal political cycle. It's a fight to the death between two models. One runs on the perception of goodness. The other runs on reality.\n\nAnd it had to be the richest man in the world. Anyone smaller would have been bought, sued or cancelled. Every billionaire before him took the deal. Elon walked out of the club and turned the lights on.\n\nHe bought Twitter for $44 billion, opened the Twitter Files and gave the world back a public square where any narrative can be checked in real time. He went inside the government and helped shut down USAID. Now Politico announces that the Democrats' gavels are coming for him. His answer: he'll find out who is really pulling the strings and go after them, not just their puppets.\n\nHe has one line he keeps coming back to: he cares about the reality of goodness, not the perception of it. That sentence is the whole war.\n\nNow the part that makes me certain of how it ends.\n\nA lie is expensive to maintain. It needs gatekeepers, censors, fact-checkers, friendly journalists, a budget, and constant repairs every time reality contradicts it. The truth costs nothing to maintain. It just keeps being true.\n\nAI is driving the cost of checking anything toward zero. Any budget, any study, any statistic, any promise, audited in seconds by anyone with a phone. Yes, AI can generate lies at scale too. But producing a lie is cheap. Defending it against a billion instant audits is impossible.\n\nThat's what acceleration really means. It doesn't pick sides. It speeds up feedback loops. Systems aligned with reality compound. Systems built on lies hit the wall faster. And you cannot build rockets, design chips or cure diseases with a false model of the world. Physics doesn't read op-eds. The more intelligence we pour into the world, the more expensive every lie becomes.\n\nYou cannot scale a lie. You can only scale what's true.\n\nWe are close to the collapse of the old order and the birth of a new one. Not the one from the WEF's own 2016 video, \"you'll own nothing and you'll be happy.\" The opposite. An order built on truth-seeking, efficiency, science, private property and free markets. You will own things. You will build things. And you'll be happy, because it will be real.\n\nGramsci's long march took a century. Its collapse will take years.\n\nHistory will remember this moment.
